## Amos - Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_ih42)

Introduction to Amos
====================

Part 1: General Introduction
----------------------------

### Outline of Amos

1. Amos introduced (1:1\)
2. Yahweh judges the surrounding nations (1:2–2:3\), the southern kingdom of Israel (2:4–5\), and the northern kingdom (2:6–16\)
3. Yahweh condemns Israel’s injustice and religious hypocrisy (3–6\)
4. Yahweh warns of coming judgment through visions (7:1–9:10\)
5. Yahweh promises restoration and renewal for his people (9:11–15\)

### What is the Book of Amos about?

The Book of Amos contains the words of Amos, a shepherd from Tekoa. Amos began to prophesy about 760 BC. He spoke Yahweh’s messages against God’s people behaving wickedly. He spoke messages to both the southern kingdom of Judah and the northern kingdom of Israel.

Yahweh gave Amos prophecies of judgment (Chapters 1–6\) to proclaim to the people.

Three visions of Yahweh coming to judge the people are in the last part of the book (7:1–9:10\). A final vision promises that Yahweh will restore Israel (9:11–15\). He will restore the “tent of David.” This means a descendant of David would once again be king over Israel.

### How should the title of this book be translated?

The traditional title of this book is “The Book of Amos” or just “Amos.” Translators may also call it the “The Book of the Sayings of Amos.”

### Who wrote the Book of Amos?

The prophet Amos probably wrote this book. He lived in the southern kingdom of Judah. Amos came from a poor family. They grew sycamore trees (7:14–15\) and were shepherds (1:1\). Though Amos was not trained as a prophet, he knew and understood the law of Moses.

Part 2: Important Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------------------------

### How is justice described in Amos?

Justice is an important theme in the Book of Amos. Justice means people treating others fairly according to the law of Yahweh. People in Israel were oppressing and taking advantage of poor people, orphans, and widows. Amos explained that Yahweh would prefer that the people act justly rather than sacrifice to him. Truly obeying the law of Moses meant being just to other people.

### What is a lawsuit?

Many cultures have a process for resolving disputes through the use of courts. These legal disputes are called lawsuits. Amos uses various legal terms. Part of the book presents events in a courtroom. The people are introduced, the problem is explained, the people are examined, witnesses speak, and a verdict is given.

Part 3: Important Translation Issues
------------------------------------

### What is the meaning of the term “Israel?”

The name “Israel” is used in many different ways in the Bible. Jacob was a son of Isaac. God changed Jacob’s name to Israel. The descendants of Jacob became a nation also called Israel. Eventually, the nation of Israel split into two kingdoms. The northern kingdom was named Israel. The southern kingdom was named Judah. In Amos, “Israel” almost always refers to the northern kingdom of Israel.

### Where do the various narratives begin and end?

The structure of the Book of Amos may make it difficult to understand where Amos ends one thought and begins another. It may be helpful to solve these issues with carefully divided lines or paragraphs.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 0:0


## Amos - Chapter 1 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_o593)

Amos 1 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh announces judgment on the nations neighboring Israel.

1. Yahweh judges the nations for their violence and cruelty (3–5\)
2. Yahweh judges the nations for their oppression and betrayal (6–10\)
3. Yahweh judges the nations for their violence and hostility (11–15\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Oracles against the nations

Each oracle repeats a fixed formula: a messenger formula, the count of the transgressions, the nation’s offense, and a sentence of fire on its fortresses. Translate these repeated phrases consistently.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 1:0

## Amos 1:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_soxp)

יְהוָה֙ מִ⁠צִיּ֣וֹן יִשְׁאָ֔ג וּ⁠מִ⁠ירוּשָׁלִַ֖ם יִתֵּ֣ן קוֹל֑⁠וֹ

**"Yahweh roars from Zion, and from Jerusalem he gives his voice"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could connect the phrases with a word other than **and** in order to show that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh roars from Zion, indeed, from Jerusalem he gives his voice]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 1:2
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Carmel (ID: `place:Carmel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

**"Yahweh roars from Zion"**

Amos is speaking of Yahweh as if he were a lion that **roars**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh thunders loudly from Zion]

## Amos 1:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_otv2)

עַל־שְׁלֹשָׁה֙ פִּשְׁעֵ֣י דַמֶּ֔שֶׂק וְ⁠עַל־אַרְבָּעָ֖ה

**"For three transgressions of Damascus, and for four"**

Yahweh is using the formula **For three transgressions of …, and for four** throughout this chapter as a traditional Hebrew poetic device meaning complete or overflowing guilt. It does not mean a literal count of four specific sins. The number three shows a full measure, and the fourth pushes past the limit to trigger God’s judgment. If this would not be clear to your readers, your language may have another way of saying this. Alternate translation: \[For all the many wrong things Damascus has done]

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`); Threshing-Sledge (ID: `realia:Threshing-sledge`)

## Amos 1:3 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_v0o5)

דּוּשָׁ֛⁠ם \& אֶת־הַ⁠גִּלְעָֽד

**"their threshing the Gilead"**

Yahweh is speaking of the way the people of Damascus attacked the people of Gilead as if they had been **threshing** them or crushing the grain they harvested. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[their savagely crushing the people of Gilead]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 1:3 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_ni45)

בַּ⁠חֲרֻצ֥וֹת הַ⁠בַּרְזֶ֖ל

**"with the threshing sledges of iron"**

The agricultural tools described here as **threshing sledges of iron** were heavy wooden boards studded with sharp iron points or stones dragged over grain to crush it. If your readers would not be familiar with what a threshing sledge is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[with threshing instruments made of iron]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos - Chapter 2 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_uaym)

Amos 2 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh judges the nations and Israel for sin.

1. Yahweh judges Judah and the nations for rebellion (1–5\)
2. Yahweh condemns Israel for injustice and oppression (6–8\)
3. Yahweh reminds Israel of his faithfulness and announces judgment (9–16\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Prophets and Nazirites

Israel silenced the prophets Yahweh raised up and made the Nazirites break their vow.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### “For three transgressions … and for four”

This traditional Hebrew poetic device continues as in chapter 1 which means complete or overflowing guilt.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:0


## Amos 2:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_lpp6)

עַל־שְׁלֹשָׁה֙ פִּשְׁעֵ֣י מוֹאָ֔ב וְ⁠עַל־אַרְבָּעָ֖ה

**"For three transgressions of Moab, and for four"**

Yahweh is using the formula **For three transgressions of …, and for four** throughout this chapter as a traditional Hebrew poetic device meaning complete or overflowing guilt. It does not mean a literal count of four specific sins. The number three shows a full measure, and the fourth pushes past the limit to trigger God’s judgment. If this would not be clear to your readers, your language may have another way of saying this. Alternate translation: \[For all the many wrong things Moab has done]

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Edom (ID: `place:Edom`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 2:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_k2v7)

עַל־שָׂרְפ֛⁠וֹ

**"because of his burning"**

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Edom (ID: `place:Edom`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 2:1 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_e8a4)

שָׂרְפ֛⁠וֹ עַצְמ֥וֹת

**"his burning the bones"**

In this culture, to burn someone’s bones was a terrible insult to the person. It involved violating his grave, and the burning was thought to disturb the person’s peace in the afterlife. You could include this information in a footnote if that would be helpful to your readers.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 2:1 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_ma6m)

לַ⁠שִּֽׂיד

**"to lime"**

By **to lime**, Yahweh means that the king of Edom’s bones were burned so completely that they turned to white ashes. You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[to white ashes]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Edom (ID: `place:Edom`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 2:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_itqs)

וְ⁠אָכְלָ֖ה אַרְמְנ֣וֹת הַ⁠קְּרִיּ֑וֹת

**"and it will devour the citadels of Kerioth"**

Yahweh is speaking of fire completely destroying the citadels in Moab’s capital city, Kerioth, as if it would **devour** them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and it will completely destroy the citadels of Kerioth]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kiriathaim (ID: `place:Kiriathaim`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 2:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_i9fg)

וּ⁠מֵ֤ת \& מוֹאָ֔ב

**"and Moab will die"**

**Moab** represents the people of Moab. Alternate translation: \[and the people who live in Moab will die]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kiriathaim (ID: `place:Kiriathaim`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 2:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_qyu1)

בְּ⁠ק֥וֹל שׁוֹפָֽר

**"with the sound of the shofar"**

A **shofar** is a kind of horn, made from the horn of a ram, that people blew to give signals in battle. If your readers would not be familiar with what a shofar is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[with the blast of a signal horn]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kiriathaim (ID: `place:Kiriathaim`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 2:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_hfeu)

וְ⁠הִכְרַתִּ֥י שׁוֹפֵ֖ט

**"And I will cut off the judge"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of removing this ruling judge of Moab as if he **will cut off** this ruler like a person would cut a branch off of a tree. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And I will remove the judge]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 2:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_wm7p)

מִ⁠קִּרְבָּ֑⁠הּ

**"from her midst"**

The pronoun **her** refers to Moab. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[from the midst of Moab]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:3
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

Here, **Judah** represents the people who live in the kingdom of Judah. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[of those who live in Judah]

## Amos 2:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_a1d4)

אֶת־תּוֹרַ֣ת יְהוָ֗ה

**"the law of Yahweh"**

Yahweh is using this possessive form to mean the **law of Yahweh** since he himself gave it to the people of Judah. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the law that Yahweh commanded them]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:4
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 2:4 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_bjfp)

אֶת־תּוֹרַ֣ת יְהוָ֗ה וְ⁠חֻקָּי⁠ו֙ לֹ֣א שָׁמָ֔רוּ

**"the law of Yahweh, and they did not keep his statutes"**

Yahweh is speaking of himself in the third person as **Yahweh** and **his**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[my law, and they did not keep my statutes]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:4
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 2:4 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_j9px)

כִּזְבֵי⁠הֶ֔ם

**"and their lies"**

Yahweh is speaking of the false gods that the people of Judah worshiped as if they were **lies**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[their idols]

וַ⁠יַּתְעוּ⁠ם֙

**"led them astray"**

Yahweh is speaking of their being deceived into disobeying God’s laws as if they were being physically **led** to go the wrong way or **astray**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[deceived them]

אֲשֶׁר־הָלְכ֥וּ אֲבוֹתָ֖⁠ם אַחֲרֵי⁠הֶֽם

**"which their fathers walked after them"**

It might seem that the expression **which their fathers walked after them** contains extra information that would not be natural to express in your language. If so, you could shorten it. Alternate translation: \[which their fathers walked after]

**"the righteous"**

Yahweh is using the adjective **righteous** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[those people who are righteous]

## Amos 2:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_xr7c)

וְ⁠אֶבְי֖וֹן

**"and the needy"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. He means that people in Israel are also selling **the needy** for a pair of sandals. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and their selling the needy]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:6
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

וְ⁠אֶבְי֖וֹן

**"and the needy"**

Yahweh is using the adjective **needy** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[and those people who are in need]

## Amos 2:6 (#7) (id: en_tn_AMO_xzlj)

בַּ⁠עֲב֥וּר נַעֲלָֽיִם

**"in exchange for a pair of sandals"**

Yahweh assumes that readers will know that **a pair of sandals** would only cost a very small amount. This means that they sold needy people into slavery for a price as low as the price of a pair of sandals. You could say that explicitly if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[for as little as the price of a pair of sandals]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:6
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Amos 2:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_tbrk)

הַ⁠שֹּׁאֲפִ֤ים עַל־עֲפַר־אֶ֨רֶץ֙ בְּ⁠רֹ֣אשׁ דַּלִּ֔ים

**"the ones trampling on the head of the poor to the dust of the earth"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of the people of Israel oppressing and mistreating the poor as if they were **trampling on the head of the poor to the dust of the earth**, crushing them underfoot into the ground. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[cruelly oppressing the poor as if they are crushing their heads into the dust]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Poor (ID: `keyterm:Poor`)

## Amos 2:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_vop1)

וְ⁠דֶ֥רֶךְ עֲנָוִ֖ים יַטּ֑וּ

**"and they turn aside the way of the afflicted"**

Yahweh is speaking of the denying justice to afflicted people as if they would **turn aside** from the **way** along which they should walk. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and they deny justice to the afflicted]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Poor (ID: `keyterm:Poor`)

## Amos 2:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_off7)

וְ⁠אִ֣ישׁ וְ⁠אָבִ֗י⁠ו יֵֽלְכוּ֙ אֶל־הַֽ⁠נַּעֲרָ֔ה

**"and a man and his father go to the girl"**

Yahweh is referring to sexual relations in a polite way by using the phrase **go to**. Depending on what is helpful for your readers, you could use a polite way of referring to this in your language, or you could state this plainly. Alternate translation: \[and a man and his father have sexual relations with the same girl]

See: Euphemism

* **Associated Articles:** Euphemism (ID: `33268@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"the afflicted"**

Yahweh is using the adjective **afflicted** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[afflicted people]

**"the name of my holiness"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe a **name** that is characterized by **holiness**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[my holy name]

**"the name of my holiness"**

**"garments taken in pledge"**

## Amos 2:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_q7a5)

אֱלֹהֵי⁠הֶֽם

**"their God"**

Yahweh is not using this possessive form **their God** to indicate that the Israelites possess or control Yahweh but rather to emphasize that Yahweh is the God that the Israelites worship. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the God whom they worship]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:8
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 2:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_w82k)

וְ⁠אָ֨נֹכִ֜י הִשְׁמַ֤דְתִּי

**"But I, I destroyed"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I destroyed** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that the Israelites could not have destroyed the Amorites without him but rather, he was the one who did it. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[It was I who destroyed]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 2:9
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

הֶעֱלֵ֥יתִי אֶתְ⁠כֶ֖ם

**"I brought you up"**

Even though Yahweh has been speaking about the people of Israel with third person pronouns, here he switches to speaking directly to them much more personally in the second person as **you**. ## Amos - Chapter 3 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_k4ll)

Amos 3 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh holds Israel accountable for rejecting him.

1. Yahweh declares Israel accountable because he chose them (1–2\)
2. Yahweh warns of coming judgment and calls his people to listen (3–10\)
3. Yahweh announces the destruction of Israel’s wealth and false security (11–15\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The horns of the altar

These projections at the corners of an altar were grasped for refuge; cutting them off (verse 14\) means there is no refuge.

### Rhetorical questions

The prophet Amos uses a chain of rhetorical questions to illustrate that God’s judgment on Israel is the expected outcome. Just as everyday causes naturally produce predictable effects, Israel’s persistent sin makes divine disaster an absolute certainty.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:0


## Amos 3:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_ukmb)

עֲלֵי⁠כֶ֖ם בְּנֵ֣י יִשְׂרָאֵ֑ל

**"against you, sons of Israel"**

Amos is speaking of the people of Israel as if they were the **sons** of their ancestor Israel. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[against you Israelites]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 3:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_uidn)

כָּל־הַ⁠מִּשְׁפָּחָ֔ה

**"all of the family"**

Here, **all of the family** represents all of the descendants of those who were captive in Egypt. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[all of you descendants of those]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

אֲשֶׁ֧ר הֶעֱלֵ֛יתִי

**"that I brought up"**

Amos first refers to **Yahweh** and then he uses the first person **I** to describe what Yahweh had done. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use the second person throughout this verse as in the UST.

* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_nvqh)

רַ֚ק אֶתְ⁠כֶ֣ם יָדַ֔עְתִּי

**"Only you have I known"**

The implication is that when Yahweh says that he has **known** Israel, he means that he chose this people to belong to him in a special covenant relationship. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Only you have I chosen to be in a covenant relationship with me]

## Amos 3:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_kt0y)

מִשְׁפְּח֣וֹת הָ⁠אֲדָמָ֑ה

**"the families of the ground"**

Yahweh is using this possessive form to mean the nations or people groups that live on the earth. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the people groups that live on the earth]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:2
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`)

## Amos 3:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_clv0)

אֵ֖ת כָּל־עֲוֺנֹֽתֵי⁠כֶֽם

**"all of your iniquities"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **iniquities**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[everything you have done wrong]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:2
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`)

## Amos 3:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_k2f6)

אֶפְקֹ֣ד עֲלֵי⁠כֶ֔ם

**"I will visit upon you"**

Yahweh is using **I will visit upon you** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I will punish you.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will punish you for]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:2
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`)

## Amos 3:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_hr6t)

הֲ⁠יֵלְכ֥וּ שְׁנַ֖יִם יַחְדָּ֑ו בִּלְתִּ֖י אִם־נוֹעָֽדוּ

**"Will two walk together, unless they have agreed"**

Yahweh, through Amos, is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that two people do not travel together unless they have arranged to do so. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Certainly two will not walk together, unless they have agreed!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:3
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 3:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_cujh)

בִּלְתִּ֖י אִם־נוֹעָֽדוּ

**"unless they have agreed"**

The pronoun **they** refers to the two people who agreed to walk together. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[unless the two of them have agreed]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:3
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 3:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_mf8o)

הֲ⁠יִשְׁאַ֤ג אַרְיֵה֙ בַּ⁠יַּ֔עַר וְ⁠טֶ֖רֶף אֵ֣ין ל֑⁠וֹ

**"Will a lion roar in the forest when there is no prey for him"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that a lion would certainly only roar if it were attacking its prey to catch and eat. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[A lion never roars in the forest when there is no prey for him]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:4
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`)

## Amos 3:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_nycj)

הֲ⁠יִתֵּ֨ן כְּפִ֤יר קוֹל⁠וֹ֙ מִ⁠מְּעֹ֣נָת֔⁠וֹ בִּלְתִּ֖י אִם־לָכָֽד

**"Will a young lion give his voice from his den, unless he has caught {something"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that a young lion growls in its den only when it has caught something. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[A young lion never gives his voice from his den unless he has caught something!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:4
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`)

## Amos 3:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_gm03)

הֲ⁠תִפֹּ֤ל צִפּוֹר֙ עַל־פַּ֣ח הָ⁠אָ֔רֶץ וּ⁠מוֹקֵ֖שׁ אֵ֣ין לָ֑⁠הּ

**"Will a bird fall into a snare {on} the ground when there is no trap for it"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that a bird is caught only when someone has set a trap for it. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[A bird will certainly not fall into a snare on the ground when there is no trap for it!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:5
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bird (ID: `fauna:Bird`); Trap (ID: `realia:Trap`)

## Amos 3:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_c1gy)

הֲ⁠יַֽעֲלֶה־פַּח֙ מִן־הָ֣⁠אֲדָמָ֔ה וְ⁠לָכ֖וֹד לֹ֥א יִלְכּֽוֹד

**"Will a snare spring up from the ground, when capturing, it will not capture"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that a trap springs up only when it has caught something. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[A snare will certainly not spring up from the ground unless it has something to capture!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:5
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bird (ID: `fauna:Bird`); Trap (ID: `realia:Trap`)

## Amos 3:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_nyee)

וְ⁠לָכ֖וֹד לֹ֥א יִלְכּֽוֹד

**"when capturing, it will not capture"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[when it has nothing to capture]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:5
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bird (ID: `fauna:Bird`); Trap (ID: `realia:Trap`)

## Amos 3:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_wd5i)

אִם־יִתָּקַ֤ע שׁוֹפָר֙ בְּ⁠עִ֔יר וְ⁠עָ֖ם לֹ֣א יֶחֱרָ֑דוּ

**"Or a shofar is blown in a city, and the people do not tremble"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that people will be afraid when they hear a warning that an enemy is attacking. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Certainly the people will tremble when a shofar is blown in a city!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 3:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_ijca)

יִתָּקַ֤ע שׁוֹפָר֙ בְּ⁠עִ֔יר

**"a shofar is blown in a city"**

Yahweh assumes that readers will know that **a shofar is blown in a city** it is to warn the people of that city that enemies are attacking. You could say that explicitly if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[an alarm is sounded in a city to warn of attacking enemies]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 3:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_i5jq)

יִתָּקַ֤ע שׁוֹפָר֙

**"a shofar is blown"**

The expression **a shofar is blown** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[someone blows a shofar]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 3:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_joar)

שׁוֹפָר֙

**"a shofar"**

A **shofar** is a kind of horn that people made from the horn of a ram and blew as a signal. If your readers would not be familiar with what a shofar is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[a warning horn]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 3:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_cyey)

תִּהְיֶ֤ה רָעָה֙ בְּ⁠עִ֔יר וַ⁠יהוָ֖ה לֹ֥א עָשָֽׂה

**"disaster happens in a city, and Yahweh has not done {it"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the certainty of the fact that Yahweh can cause disasters to happen as punishment. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate these questions as statements or as exclamations. Alternate translation: \[when disaster happens in a city, it is Yahweh who has done it!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 3:6 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_aijy)

וַ⁠יהוָ֖ה לֹ֥א עָשָֽׂה

**"and Yahweh has not done {it"**

Here and in the next couple of verses, it could be that: (1\) Amos is speaking about Yahweh as modeled in the UST (end Yahweh’s quote at the end of verse 2\) or (2\) Yahweh is speaking of himself in the third person. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person throughout these verses. Alternate translation: \[and I, Yahweh have not done it]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`)

## Amos 3:6 (#7) (id: en_tn_AMO_bk6i)

וַ⁠יהוָ֖ה לֹ֥א עָשָֽׂה

**"and Yahweh has not done {it"**

The pronoun **it** refers to the disaster that happens in the city. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and Yahweh has not caused that disaster]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:6
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_iwn2)

לֹ֧א יַעֲשֶׂ֛ה \& דָּבָ֑ר

**"will not do a thing"**

By **Lord Yahweh will not do a thing**, means Yahweh will not bring any judgment to those he has a covenant relationship with, without first telling his prophets who serve him so they can warn the people what will happen if they do not turn from their sinful acts. You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[will not bring judgment]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:7
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 3:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_tgtb)

אֲדֹנָ֥⁠י יְהוִ֖ה

**"the Lord Yahweh"**

Here and in the next verse, it could be that: (1\) Amos is speaking about Yahweh as modeled in the UST (end Yahweh’s quote at the end of verse 2\) or (2\) Yahweh is speaking of himself in the third person. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person throughout these verses. Alternate translation: \[I, the Lord Yahweh]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:7
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_ukbf)

סוֹד֔⁠וֹ

**"his secret counsel"**

Yahweh is using this possessive form to mean the secret plans that he himself has made. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the secret plans that he has made] or \[the secret plans the I have made]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:7
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_fppa)

אַרְיֵ֥ה שָׁאָ֖ג

**"The lion has roared"**

Yahweh is speaking of the message he’s giving to the people through Amos as if it were the roar of a **lion** that everyone who hears it must fear (see 1:2\). Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh has roared like a lion]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:8
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_p9nq)

מִ֣י לֹ֣א יִירָ֑א

**"who will not fear"**

Yahweh is using the question form to state emphatically that everyone who hears a lion roar will be afraid. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[surely it would make anyone afraid!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:8
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`)

## Amos 3:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_x1us)

מִ֖י לֹ֥א יִנָּבֵֽא

**"who will not prophesy"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize the fact that when Lord Yahweh has spoken to his prophet, that prophet must proclaim his message. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[surely the one who hears him is compelled to prophesy!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:8
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_msq3)

הַשְׁמִ֨יעוּ֙

**"Cause {people} to hear"**

The implication is that the messengers are to make this proclamation to the people who live in **the citadels in Ashdod** and in **the citadels in the land of Egypt.** You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Proclaim to the people]

הַשְׁמִ֨יעוּ֙ עַל־אַרְמְנ֣וֹת בְּ⁠אַשְׁדּ֔וֹד וְ⁠עַֽל־אַרְמְנ֖וֹת

**"Cause {people} to hear on the citadels in Ashdod, and on the citadels"**

Here, **on the citadels** represents the rulers and other powerful people who live in those citadels. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[Tell those who are Ashdod’s rulers and who rule]

**"the great uproars in her midst"**

**"and the oppressed within her"**

Yahweh is using the adjective **oppressed** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[and the oppressed people within her]

**"And they do not know to do right"**

* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_dsj8)

נְאֻם־יְהוָ֑ה

**"the declaration of Yahweh"**

Amos, who is conveying Yahweh’s words, is interjecting **the declaration of Yahweh** to emphasize that what has been said is a direct quotation from Yahweh and comes with Yahweh’s authority. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"violence and destruction"**

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:10
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 3:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_recr)

חָמָ֥ס וָ⁠שֹׁ֖ד

**"violence and destruction"**

Here, **violence and destruction** represents the wealth that was attained and stored up as a result of the nation’s acting violently and destructively toward others. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[wealth gained through violence and destruction]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:10
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 3:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_wqqy)

לָ⁠כֵ֗ן

**"Therefore"**

The word translated as **Therefore** indicates that what follows is a result of what came before. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that what follows is a result of what came before. Alternate translation: \[Because they have done these things]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

## Amos 3:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_acmz)

צַ֖ר וּ⁠סְבִ֣יב הָ⁠אָ֑רֶץ

**"An adversary—and around the land"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[An adversary will come, and he will be all around the land]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:11
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:11 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_h4ha)

צַ֖ר וּ⁠סְבִ֣יב הָ⁠אָ֑רֶץ

**"An adversary—and around the land"**

The implication is that **An adversary** is an enemy army that will surround the land and attack its cities. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[The army of the adversary will besiege the land on every side]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:11 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_fwtf)

וְ⁠הוֹרִ֤ד מִמֵּ⁠ךְ֙ עֻזֵּ֔⁠ךְ

**"And he will bring down from you your strength"**

The pronoun **he** refers to the adversary, and the pronoun **you** refers to the land of Israel around Samaria and the people who live there. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And that adversary will bring down from you Israelites your strength]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:11
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:11 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_exih)

וְ⁠הוֹרִ֤ד מִמֵּ⁠ךְ֙ עֻזֵּ֔⁠ךְ

**"And he will bring down from you your strength"**

Here, **your strength** represents the fortifications that the people of Israel trusted to protect them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And he will bring down your strong defenses]

**"and your citadels will be plundered"**

The expression **be plundered** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. The adversary would be the one doing this. Alternate translation: \[and he will plunder your citadels]

## Amos 3:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_yjnc)

כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁר֩ יַצִּ֨יל הָ⁠רֹעֶ֜ה מִ⁠פִּ֧י הָ⁠אֲרִ֛י שְׁתֵּ֥י כְרָעַ֖יִם א֣וֹ בְדַל־אֹ֑זֶן כֵּ֣ן יִנָּצְל֞וּ בְּנֵ֣י יִשְׂרָאֵ֗ל

**"As the shepherd rescues from the mouth of the lion two legs or a piece of an ear, thus will the sons of Israel be rescued"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the shepherd** can save only **two legs or a piece of an ear** from a lion that has attacked a sheep, so only a small remnant of the people of Israel will survive. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[Just as the shepherd can recover only parts of a sheep from the mouth of the lion, so only a small remnant of the sons of Israel will be rescued]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:12
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Bed (ID: `realia:Bed`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`)

## Amos 3:12 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_qwvx)

בְּנֵ֣י יִשְׂרָאֵ֗ל

**"the sons of Israel"**

Here, **sons** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the sons of Israel** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as **Israel.** Alternate translation: \[the Israelites]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:12
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:12 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_hrqy)

וּ⁠בִ⁠דְמֶ֥שֶׁק עָֽרֶשׂ

**"and with a cover of a couch"**

Scholars are not sure what the word translated as **cover** actually means. It could be: (1\) an unknown piece of a couch, as in the UST, or (2\) a type of cloth, such as the city of Damascus was known for at a later time. Alternate translation: \[and on a damask of a couch] or (3\) the word “Damascus,” although spelled a little differently than usual. Alternate translation: \[and on a Damascus couch]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 3:12
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 3:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_oz6y)

נְאֻם־אֲדֹנָ֥⁠י יְהוִ֖ה אֱלֹהֵ֥י הַ⁠צְּבָאֽוֹת

**"the declaration of the Lord Yahweh, the God of Armies"**

Amos is interjecting the phrase **the declaration of Yahweh** into what Yahweh is saying to emphasize that what is about to be said is a direct quotation from Yahweh and comes with Yahweh’s authority. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

קַרְנ֣וֹת הַ⁠מִּזְבֵּ֔חַ

**"the horns of the altar"**

The top corners of the central altar at Bethel (and others) were shaped like bull horns. These horns were a symbol of the strength of the god that was worshiped there. The horns were considered the most sacred and powerful part of the altar. You could include this information in a footnote if it would be helpful to your readers.

## Amos - Chapter 4 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_on2v)

Amos 4 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh calls Israel to repentance.

1. Yahweh condemns Israel’s oppression and indulgence (1–3\)
2. Israel refuses to return to Yahweh despite his warnings (4–11\)
3. Yahweh warns Israel to prepare to meet him (12–13\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### “return” to Yahweh

Each disaster in verses 6–11 closes with the refrain “yet you did not return as far as me.” To “return” is to repent and come back to a right relationship with Yahweh.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:0


## Amos 4:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_p79y)

שִׁמְע֞וּ הַ⁠דָּבָ֣ר הַ⁠זֶּ֗ה פָּר֤וֹת הַ⁠בָּשָׁן֙

**"Listen to this word, cows of Bashan"**

Amos is speaking to the wealthy women of Samaria, whom he calls **cows of Bashan**, although he knows that they cannot hear him. He is doing this to show his listeners in a strong way how he feels about the way these women oppress the poor. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about these women. Alternate translation: \[Hear this word about the cows of Bashan]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Amos 4:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_clm4)

פָּר֤וֹת הַ⁠בָּשָׁן֙

**"cows of Bashan"**

Amos is speaking of the wealthy women of Samaria as if they were **cows of Bashan**, that is, well\-fed cattle grazing in rich pasture on the hillsides overlooking Samaria. If it would be clearer in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[you well\-fed, pampered women]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Amos 4:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_n3jv)

הָ⁠רֹצְצ֖וֹת

**"the ones crushing"**

Amos is speaking of the cruel way that these wealthy women are mistreating the needy people as if they were cattle **crushing** them underfoot. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the ones mistreating the needy]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Amos 4:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_nv7z)

דַּלִּ֔ים

**"the poor"**

Amos is using the adjective **poor** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[those who are poor]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

אֶבְיוֹנִ֑ים

**"the needy"**

Amos is using the adjective **needy** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[people who are needy]

## Amos 4:1 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_kbp7)

הָ⁠אֹמְרֹ֥ת לַ⁠אֲדֹֽנֵי⁠הֶ֖ם

**"the ones saying to their masters"**

The implication is that the **masters** of these women are their husbands whom they should be serving. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the ones saying to their husbands]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

## Amos 4:1 (#7) (id: en_tn_AMO_l0me)

לַ⁠אֲדֹֽנֵי⁠הֶ֖ם הָבִ֥יאָ⁠ה וְ⁠נִשְׁתֶּֽה

**"to their masters, “Bring, and let us drink"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you could express this as an indirect quotation. Alternate translation: \[to their masters that they should bring them something to drink]

## Amos 4:1 (#8) (id: en_tn_AMO_jnes)

הָבִ֥יאָ⁠ה וְ⁠נִשְׁתֶּֽה

**"Bring, and let us drink"**

The women are leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[Bring drinks to us]

See: Ellipsis

## Amos 4:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_tlq2)

נִשְׁבַּ֨ע אֲדֹנָ֤⁠י יְהוִה֙ בְּ⁠קָדְשׁ֔⁠וֹ

**"The Lord Yahweh has sworn by his holiness"**

Following the custom of the culture, the Lord Yahweh is swearing an oath and guaranteeing it by something sacred, his own **holiness**. Use a natural way in your language to express an oath. Alternate translation: \[The Lord Yahweh has solemnly promised, as surely as he is holy]

See: Oath Formulas

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fishhook (ID: `realia:Fishhook`)

## Amos 4:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_aoyo)

בְּ⁠קָדְשׁ֔⁠וֹ

**"by his holiness"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **holiness**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[by his holy name]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fishhook (ID: `realia:Fishhook`)

## Amos 4:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_muck)

כִּ֛י הִנֵּ֥ה יָמִ֖ים

**"that, behold, the days"**

Amos did not always say who was speaking or who was being addressed. Here, it seems to be the beginning of what Yahweh said but the ULT shows it as Amos telling us what Yahweh said (no quote marks). If it would be helpful to your readers, you could mark this as a direct quote of Yahweh as in the UST.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fishhook (ID: `realia:Fishhook`)

## Amos 4:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_b7hr)

כִּ֛י הִנֵּ֥ה יָמִ֖ים

**"that, behold, the days"**

Yahweh is using the term **behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[and you should take notice that the days]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fishhook (ID: `realia:Fishhook`)

## Amos 4:2 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_mmnm)

וְ⁠נִשָּׂ֤א אֶתְ⁠כֶם֙

**"when he will lift you up"**

The pronoun **he** refers to their enemies and **you** to the women of Samaria. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[when your enemies will lift you women up]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fishhook (ID: `realia:Fishhook`)

## Amos 4:2 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_khpi)

וְ⁠נִשָּׂ֤א אֶתְ⁠כֶם֙ בְּ⁠צִנּ֔וֹת וְ⁠אַחֲרִיתְ⁠כֶ֖ן בְּ⁠סִיר֥וֹת דּוּגָֽה

**"when he will lift you up with hooks, and the last of you with hooks of fishery"**

Amos is speaking of the women being carried away as captives as if they were fish that someone catches **with hooks** or **hooks of fishery.** If it would be clearer in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[when he will carry you away as captives, every last one of you, as easily as a fisherman uses hooks to catch fish]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fishhook (ID: `realia:Fishhook`)

## Amos 4:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_d8h1)

אִשָּׁ֣ה

**"a woman"**

Amos is using the phrase **a woman** as a common expression of the culture to mean “each woman.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[each woman]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

נֶגְדָּ֑⁠הּ

**"in front of her"**

Amos is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[going out through the break in the wall that is in front of her]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 4:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_x4cy)

נְאֻם־יְהוָֽה

**"the declaration of Yahweh"**

Amos is interjecting the phrase **the declaration of Yahweh** to indicate that what is being said comes with Yahweh’s authority.

* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 4:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_tpnd)

וְ⁠הִשְׁלַכְתֶּ֥נָה הַ⁠הַרְמ֖וֹנָ⁠ה

**"and you will throw {yourselves} out toward Harmon"**

The word translated as **toward Harmon** could be: (1\) the name of a place that is now unknown. (2\) a word that means “rule”or “dominion.” Alternate translation: \[and you will cast away dominion] or \[and you will let go of your rule]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Harmon (ID: `place:Harmon`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 4:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_sk21)

בֹּ֤אוּ בֵֽית־אֵל֙ וּ⁠פִשְׁע֔וּ הַ⁠גִּלְגָּ֖ל הַרְבּ֣וּ לִ⁠פְשֹׁ֑עַ

**"Go to Bethel and transgress, to Gilgal and multiply to transgress"**

Yahweh actually means to communicate the opposite of the literal meaning of what he is saying here and throughout the next verse. He is telling them that they are doing these rituals with the wrong motives and thus sinning. If a speaker of your language would not do that, you could translate the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[You should not go to Bethel and sin there, nor to Gilgal and sin even more]

See: Irony

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:4
* **Associated Articles:** Irony (ID: `33288@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Gilgal (ID: `place:Gilgal.2`); Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 4:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_iak2)

וּ⁠פִשְׁע֔וּ

**"and transgress"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **transgress**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and go against Yahweh’s commands]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

לִ⁠שְׁלֹ֥שֶׁת יָמִ֖ים מַעְשְׂרֹֽתֵי⁠כֶֽם

**"your tithes every three days"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and bring your tithes every three days]

## Amos 4:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_a7tf)

וְ⁠קַטֵּ֤ר מֵֽ⁠חָמֵץ֙ תּוֹדָ֔ה וְ⁠קִרְא֥וּ נְדָב֖וֹת הַשְׁמִ֑יעוּ

**"And make a thank offering smoke from what is leavened, and call out freewill offerings; make {people} hear"**

Yahweh actually means to communicate the opposite of the literal meaning of what he is saying here. If a speaker of your language would not do that, you could translate the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I take no pleasure when you make a thank offering from what is leavened, and loudly announce your freewill offerings]

See: Irony

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Irony (ID: `33288@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Praise (ID: `keyterm:Praise`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 4:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_vjre)

וְ⁠קַטֵּ֤ר מֵֽ⁠חָמֵץ֙ תּוֹדָ֔ה

**"And make a thank offering smoke from what is leavened"**

Amos assumes that readers will know that Yahweh had forbidden any burning of bread that contains yeast as a sacrifice to him (Leviticus 2:11\). You could include this information in a footnote if that would be helpful to your readers.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Praise (ID: `keyterm:Praise`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 4:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_dtc3)

וְ⁠קִרְא֥וּ נְדָב֖וֹת

**"and call out freewill offerings"**

The phrase **call out freewill offerings** could mean: (1\) that people were to publicly announce when they brought a gift to Yahweh. This would be against the purpose of these offerings which were to be given whenever someone wanted to give something to Yahweh, Alternate translation: \[and announce that you are bringing a freewill offering] or (2\) that people would be required to bring freewill offerings. This would be the opposite of the meaning of freewill offerings, but perhaps what the people were doing. Alternate translation: \[and announce that I require freewill offerings]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Praise (ID: `keyterm:Praise`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 4:5 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_upxn)

כִּ֣י כֵ֤ן אֲהַבְתֶּם֙ בְּנֵ֣י יִשְׂרָאֵ֔ל

**"For thus you have loved, sons of Israel"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you could move this phrase to the beginning of verse 4, since **For thus you have loved** gives the reason for these instructions that Yahweh is giving the people that are contradictory to what he has actually commanded them to do. Alternate translation: \[Since you so love to do these things, sons of Israel, go ahead to Bethel]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Praise (ID: `keyterm:Praise`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 4:5 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_hsuu)

בְּנֵ֣י יִשְׂרָאֵ֔ל

**"sons of Israel"**

Amos is speaking as if the Israelites were actually the **sons** of the patriarch **Israel**, who was also known as Jacob. Alternate translation: \[you people of Israel]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

נְאֻ֖ם אֲדֹנָ֥⁠י יְהוִֽה

**"the declaration of the Lord Yahweh"**

## Amos 4:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_fbf4)

וְ⁠גַם־אֲנִי֩ נָתַ֨תִּי לָ⁠כֶ֜ם

**"And even I, I gave to you"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I gave** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that he himself is the one who sent the famine. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[And even I myself gave to you]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 4:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_mxq2)

נָתַ֨תִּי לָ⁠כֶ֜ם נִקְי֤וֹן שִׁנַּ֨יִם֙

**"I gave to you cleanness of teeth"**

Yahweh is using **cleanness of teeth** as a common expression of the culture to mean “famine,” since people who have nothing to eat do not dirty their teeth. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I caused you to have famine]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 4:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_xqjr)

נִקְי֤וֹן שִׁנַּ֨יִם֙ בְּ⁠כָל־עָ֣רֵי⁠כֶ֔ם וְ⁠חֹ֣סֶר לֶ֔חֶם בְּ⁠כֹ֖ל מְקוֹמֹֽתֵי⁠כֶ֑ם

**"cleanness of teeth in all your cities, and lack of bread in all your places"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way as in the UST. Another approach would be to connect the phrases with a word that shows that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. Alternate translation: \[cleanness of teeth in all your cities, indeed I gave to you lack of bread in all your places]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 4:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_r8qr)

וְ⁠חֹ֣סֶר לֶ֔חֶם

**"and lack of bread"**

Yahweh is using one kind of food, **bread**, to mean food in general. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and lack of any food]

See: Synecdoche

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Synecdoche (ID: `33315@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 4:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_v8ch)

וְ⁠לֹֽא־שַׁבְתֶּ֥ם עָדַ֖⁠י

**"yet you did not return as far as me"**

Yahweh is speaking of the people repenting and giving him their loyalty again as if they would **return** to him along a road they were traveling on. If it would be clearer in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[yet you did not repent and give me your full allegiance]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 4:6 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_uszd)

וְ⁠לֹֽא־שַׁבְתֶּ֥ם

**"yet you did not return"**

Yahweh uses the word **yet** here to indicate a contrast between the famine that he sent and the response that he expected from the people. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[but in spite of that, you did not return]

See: Connect — Contrast Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Contrast Relationship (ID: `33329@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Innocence (ID: `keyterm:Innocence`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

וְ⁠גַ֣ם אָנֹכִי֩ מָנַ֨עְתִּי מִ⁠כֶּ֜ם

**"And also I, I withheld from you"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I withheld** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that he himself is the one who sent the drought. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[And also I myself withheld from you]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

## Amos 4:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_jflh)

וְ⁠הִמְטַרְתִּי֙ עַל־עִ֣יר אֶחָ֔ת וְ⁠עַל־עִ֥יר אַחַ֖ת לֹ֣א אַמְטִ֑יר חֶלְקָ֤ה אַחַת֙ תִּמָּטֵ֔ר וְ⁠חֶלְקָ֛ה

**"And I made it rain on one city, and on one city I did not make it rain; one portion was rained on, and a portion"**

Yahweh uses the word **and** here to indicate a contrast between the city on which he made it rain and the city on which he did not make it rain. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And I made it rain on one city, but on another city I did not make it rain; one portion was rained on, but another portion]

See: Connect — Contrast Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Contrast Relationship (ID: `33329@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 4:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_zpyf)

חֶלְקָ֤ה אַחַת֙ תִּמָּטֵ֔ר

**"one portion was rained on"**

The expression **was rained on** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[I sent rain on one portion]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 4:7 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_bbf4)

וְ⁠חֶלְקָ֛ה אֲשֶֽׁר־לֹֽא־תַמְטִ֥יר עָלֶ֖י⁠הָ

**"and a portion which it did not rain on it"**

It might seem that the expression **a portion which it did not rain on it** contains extra information that would not be natural to express in your language. If so, you could shorten it. Alternate translation: \[and another portion that received no rain]

See: Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit (ID: `33274@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 4:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_ygbq)

וְ⁠נָע֡וּ שְׁתַּיִם֩ שָׁלֹ֨שׁ עָרִ֜ים

**"And two–three cities staggered"**

Yahweh is using **two–three cities** as a common expression of the culture to mean a small but indefinite number of cities. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And a few cities staggered]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

**"two–three cities"**

## Amos 4:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_bjis)

הָפַ֣כְתִּי בָ⁠כֶ֗ם כְּ⁠מַהְפֵּכַ֤ת אֱלֹהִים֙ אֶת־סְדֹ֣ם וְ⁠אֶת־עֲמֹרָ֔ה

**"I overthrew among you as God overthrew Sodom and Gomorrah"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **God overthrew Sodom and Gomorrah** so completely that nothing was left of them, so the destruction that Yahweh brought on the Israelites was devastating. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[I brought devastating destruction among you, just as God so completely destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah that nothing was left of them]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 4:11
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`)

## Amos 4:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_k45i)

וַ⁠תִּהְי֕וּ כְּ⁠א֖וּד מֻצָּ֣ל מִ⁠שְׂרֵפָ֑ה

**"and you were like a firebrand snatched from a burning"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a firebrand snatched from a burning** is charred and barely saved, so the Israelites who survived were rescued only barely and at great cost. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and some of you barely survived, as though you had been pulled out of a fire at the last moment]

## Amos 4:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_oqaz)

כִּ֡י הִנֵּה֩

**"For behold"**

The word translated as **For** connects this description of Yahweh to what he has just said, that they should “prepare to meet” their God, and the word **behold** calls attention to what follows. If the connection between this statement and the previous one is not clear, you may want to use a different connecting word to show how this statement relates to what comes before it. Alternate translation: \[In fact, you will need to prepare to meet your God by carefully considering that he is]

## Amos - Chapter 5 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_iix6)

Amos 5 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Yahweh mourns Israel’s coming downfall (1–3\)
2. Yahweh calls Israel to seek him and reject injustice (4–17\)
3. Yahweh warns of the coming day of Yahweh (18–20\)
4. Yahweh rejects empty worship and demands justice (21–27\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The gate

Elders decided legal cases at the city gate, so the wrongs in 5:10 and 5:12 are abuses of the courts.

### The day of Yahweh

Israel expected this “day of Yahweh” to bring victory, but it will bring judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:0

אֶת־הַ⁠דָּבָ֣ר הַ⁠זֶּ֗ה

Here, **this word** represents “the message that Yahweh is giving through Amos.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[this message]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 5:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_q6gf)

נָֽפְלָה֙ לֹֽא־תוֹסִ֣יף ק֔וּם בְּתוּלַ֖ת יִשְׂרָאֵ֑ל

**"She has fallen; she will not repeat to rise, the virgin Israel"**

Amos is speaking of the nation of Israel being vulnerable as if it were a young **virgin** woman who **has fallen** down and who will never **rise** again. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The nation of Israel has been defeated; she will never regain her power]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 5:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_xi3s)

נָֽפְלָה֙

**"She has fallen"**

The pronoun **She** refers to the nation of Israel, whom Amos names as **the virgin Israel** later in this verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[The nation of Israel has fallen]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 5:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_gayb)

אֵ֥ין מְקִימָֽ⁠הּ

**"there is no one raising her up"**

Here Amos is speaking of there being no one to help Israel as if it were a woman who had physically “fallen” and no one is **raising her up.** If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly or use a comparable expression. Alternate translation: \[there is no one to help her recover]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 5:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_l98k)

הָ⁠עִ֛יר הַ⁠יֹּצֵ֥את אֶ֖לֶף

**"The city going out a thousand"**

Here, **The city** represents “the fighting men who march out from the city.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[A thousand fighting men marching out from a city]

## Amos 5:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_rfnx)

הַ⁠יֹּצֵ֥את אֶ֖לֶף תַּשְׁאִ֣יר מֵאָ֑ה

**"going out a thousand will leave a hundred remaining"**

Here, **a thousand** and **a hundred** represent groups of soldiers. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language as modeled in the UST.

## Amos 5:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_gb2f)

דִּרְשׁ֖וּ⁠נִי וִֽ⁠חְיֽוּ

**"Seek me and live"**

Yahweh is speaking as if the people could **Seek** him, as though he were something lost that they could look for and find. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Devote yourselves to me and live]

## Amos 5:4 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_n4n2)

דִּרְשׁ֖וּ⁠נִי וִֽ⁠חְיֽוּ

**"Seek me and live"**

The word translated as **Seek** is an imperative, but it communicates a condition rather than a command. If the imperative does not communicate a condition for your readers, then use a form in your language that communicates a condition. Alternate translation: \[If you seek me, then you will live]

See: Imperatives — Other Uses

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:4
* **Associated Articles:** Imperatives — Other Uses (ID: `33284@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_rpra)

וְ⁠אַֽל־תִּדְרְשׁוּ֙ בֵּֽית־אֵ֔ל וְ⁠הַ⁠גִּלְגָּל֙ לֹ֣א תָבֹ֔אוּ וּ⁠בְאֵ֥ר שֶׁ֖בַע לֹ֣א תַעֲבֹ֑רוּ כִּ֤י הַ⁠גִּלְגָּל֙ גָּלֹ֣ה יִגְלֶ֔ה וּ⁠בֵֽית־אֵ֖ל יִהְיֶ֥ה לְ⁠אָֽוֶן

**"and do not seek Bethel, and do not go to Gilgal, and do not cross over to Beersheba; for Gilgal, going into exile, will go into exile, and Bethel will become iniquity"**

Yahweh uses the word **and** here to indicate a contrast between seeking him and seeking the shrines at Bethel, Gilgal, and Beersheba. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[but do not seek Bethel; instead of going to Gilgal or crossing over to Beersheba, remember that Gilgal will surely go into exile, and Bethel will become iniquity.]

See: Connect — Contrast Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:5
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Contrast Relationship (ID: `33329@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); Gilgal (ID: `place:Gilgal.2`); Mischief (ID: `keyterm:Mischief`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 5:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_eqjc)

וְ⁠אַֽל־תִּדְרְשׁוּ֙ בֵּֽית־אֵ֔ל

**"and do not seek Bethel"**

Yahweh is speaking of devoting themselves and worshiping at Bethel as if the people could **seek** and find something that had been lost. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and do not devote yourselves to worshiping at Bethel]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)
