בֵּֽית־אֵ֔ל

**"Bethel"**

Here, **Bethel** represents “the shrine at Bethel where the people worshiped.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the shrine at Bethel]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:5 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_t4gn)

הַ⁠גִּלְגָּל֙ גָּלֹ֣ה יִגְלֶ֔ה

**"Gilgal, going into exile, will go into exile"**

The words translated as **going into exile, will go into exile** sound like the name of the town, **Gilgal**. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could put this information in a footnote.

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:5
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); Gilgal (ID: `place:Gilgal.2`); Mischief (ID: `keyterm:Mischief`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 5:5 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_tsui)

גָּלֹ֣ה יִגְלֶ֔ה

**"going into exile, will go into exile"**

Yahweh is repeating forms of the verb **go into exile** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[for Gilgal will certainly go into exile]

See: Reduplication

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); Gilgal (ID: `place:Gilgal.2`); Mischief (ID: `keyterm:Mischief`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

**"and Bethel will become iniquity"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **iniquity**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and Bethel will become an evil place]

**"and Bethel will become iniquity"**

## Amos 5:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_vj8h)

דִּרְשׁ֥וּ אֶת־יְהוָ֖ה וִֽ⁠חְי֑וּ

**"Seek Yahweh and live"**

Amos is speaking as if the people could **Seek** Yahweh, as though he were something lost that they could look for and find. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Devote yourselves to Yahweh and live]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_e1em)

פֶּן־יִצְלַ֤ח

**"lest he break out"**

Amos is using the word translated as **lest** to introduce an outcome that he does not want to happen. Because of this, he first gives the people of Israel an instruction to follow so that this will not happen. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that avoiding this outcome is the purpose of the instruction. Alternate translation: \[otherwise, he will break out]

See: Connect — Goal (Purpose) Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Goal (Purpose) Relationship (ID: `33330@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 5:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_hy0v)

פֶּן־יִצְלַ֤ח כָּ⁠אֵשׁ֙ בֵּ֣ית יוֹסֵ֔ף

**"lest he break out like fire {in} the house of Joseph"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **fire** spreads swiftly and destroys everything in its path, so Yahweh’s judgment will come swiftly and destroy the descendants of Joseph. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[lest he break out against the house of Joseph like a fire that spreads swiftly and destroys everything in its path]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 5:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_esld)

בֵּ֣ית יוֹסֵ֔ף

**"in} the house of Joseph"**

Here the term **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the house of Joseph** means all the people descended from the patriarch Joseph. By this time, those descendants had become the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh. Alternate translation: \[among the descendants of Joseph] or \[among the people of the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 5:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_iewa)

וְ⁠אָכְלָ֥ה וְ⁠אֵין־מְכַבֶּ֖ה לְ⁠בֵֽית־אֵֽל

**"and it consumes, and there is no extinguisher for Bethel"**

is speaking as if Yahweh’s judgment against them were a **fire** that **consumes** them. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and his judgment will destroy Bethel, and no one will be able to stop it]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Amos 5:6 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_rgfs)

וְ⁠אָכְלָ֥ה

**"and it consumes"**

The pronoun **it** refers to the **fire** that Yahweh will send. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and the fire consumes]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_q852)

הַ⁠הֹפְכִ֥ים לְ⁠לַעֲנָ֖ה מִשְׁפָּ֑ט

**"the {ones} turning justice into wormwood"**

Amos is speaking to those who have distorted justice as if **justice** were something that they could change into **wormwood**, a poisonous plant with a very bitter taste. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[those who have distorted justice for people]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wormwood (ID: `flora:Wormwood`)

## Amos 5:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_f1sw)

הַ⁠הֹפְכִ֥ים לְ⁠לַעֲנָ֖ה מִשְׁפָּ֑ט

**"the {ones} turning justice into wormwood"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **justice**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the ones turning what is just into something bitter]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_mdmw)

וּ⁠צְדָקָ֖ה לָ⁠אָ֥רֶץ הִנִּֽיחוּ

**"and they cast righteousness to the ground"**

Amos is speaking of rejecting righteousness as if **righteousness** were an object that these people could **cast** down **to the ground** and leave there. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and they completely reject righteousness]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wormwood (ID: `flora:Wormwood`)

וּ⁠צְדָקָ֖ה לָ⁠אָ֥רֶץ הִנִּֽיחוּ

**"and they cast righteousness to the ground"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **righteousness**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and they cast what is right to the ground]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_jyle)

עֹשֵׂ֨ה כִימָ֜ה וּ⁠כְסִ֗יל וְ⁠הֹפֵ֤ךְ לַ⁠בֹּ֨קֶר֙ צַלְמָ֔וֶת וְ⁠י֖וֹם לַ֣יְלָה הֶחְשִׁ֑יךְ הַ⁠קּוֹרֵ֣א לְ⁠מֵֽי־הַ⁠יָּ֗ם וַֽ⁠יִּשְׁפְּכֵ֛⁠ם עַל־פְּנֵ֥י הָ⁠אָ֖רֶץ יְהוָ֥ה שְׁמֽ⁠וֹ

**"The maker of the Pleiades and Orion, turning deep darkness into morning; he darkens day into night; calling for the waters of the sea, he pours them out on the face of the earth— Yahweh is his name"**

Amos describes Yahweh as the one with power over all he created by a series of phrases and only names him at the end of the verse. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about who Yahweh is before the information about what he does. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh is the maker of the Pleiades and Orion, turning deep darkness into morning; he darkens day into night; calling for the waters of the sea, he pours them out on the face of the earth.]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_ijl2)

כִימָ֜ה וּ⁠כְסִ֗יל

**"the Pleiades and Orion"**

The terms **Pleiades** and **Orion** are the names of groups of stars in the night sky. If your readers would not be familiar with what these are, you could use the names of star groups that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[the groups of stars in the sky]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_i1oh)

וְ⁠הֹפֵ֤ךְ לַ⁠בֹּ֨קֶר֙ צַלְמָ֔וֶת וְ⁠י֖וֹם לַ֣יְלָה הֶחְשִׁ֑יךְ

**"turning deep darkness into morning; he darkens day into night"**

Amos is referring to all of time by naming the two major components, **morning** and **night**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[governing the passing of all time, from morning until night]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_srzq)

עַל־פְּנֵ֥י הָ⁠אָ֖רֶץ

**"on the face of the earth"**

Amos is using **the face of the earth** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the surface of the earth.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[everywhere on the earth]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:8 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_styb)

יְהוָ֥ה שְׁמֽ⁠וֹ

**"Yahweh is his name"**

Amos is using the term **name** to represent who the Yahweh is or to refer to the Yahweh’s reputation. Alternate translation: \[This is Yahweh himself]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_w6tv)

הַ⁠מַּבְלִ֥יג שֹׁ֖ד עַל־עָ֑ז

**"the one making destruction flash against the strong"**

Amos is speaking destroying the strong as if **destruction** were lightning that Yahweh could make **flash** suddenly against strong people. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the one bringing sudden ruin upon the strong]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 5:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_a9oh)

הַ⁠מַּבְלִ֥יג שֹׁ֖ד עַל־עָ֑ז וְ⁠שֹׁ֖ד עַל \& יָבֽוֹא

**"the one making destruction flash against the strong, and destruction comes against"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **destruction**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the one suddenly destroying the strong, and he destroys]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 5:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_z5yg)

עַל־עָ֑ז

**"against the strong"**

Amos is using the adjective **strong** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[against those who are strong]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 5:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_l9mq)

שָׂנְא֥וּ בַ⁠שַּׁ֖עַר מוֹכִ֑יחַ

**"They hate the reprover in the gate"**

The pronoun **They** refers to the strong referred to in the previous verse, or those who are influential people of Israel whom Amos is condemning. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Those of you who are strong hate the reprover in the gate]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

בַ⁠שַּׁ֖עַר מוֹכִ֑יחַ

**"the reprover in the gate"**

Here, **the gate** represents “the place where the leaders of a city decided legal cases.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the reprover at the place of judgment]

**"of your trampling on the poor"**

רַבִּ֣ים פִּשְׁעֵי⁠כֶ֔ם וַ⁠עֲצֻמִ֖ים חַטֹּֽאתֵי⁠כֶ֑ם

**"your transgressions {are} many, and your sins {are} mighty"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **transgressions** and **sins**, you could express the same ideas in another way. Alternate translation: \[that you have done many bad things and that you have sinned greatly]

## Amos 5:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_wqoo)

רַבִּ֣ים פִּשְׁעֵי⁠כֶ֔ם וַ⁠עֲצֻמִ֖ים חַטֹּֽאתֵי⁠כֶ֑ם

**"your transgressions {are} many, and your sins {are} mighty"**

The terms **transgressions** and **sins** mean similar things. Yahweh is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[your sins are very many and very great]

**"and they turn aside the needy"**

Yahweh is speaking of those who deny justice to poor people as if they physically **turn aside the needy** so that they cannot reach the **gate**, the place where legal cases are decided. ## Amos 5:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_qhdj)

הַ⁠מַּשְׂכִּ֛יל

**"the prudent"**

Amos is using the adjective **prudent** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[those who are wise]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:13
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_mgek)

יִדֹּ֑ם

**"will be still"**

The word translated as **will be still** could mean: (1\) “will be quiet.” That is, prudent people will avoid criticizing people who do evil because doing so would be futile. Alternate translation: \[will be silent] or (2\) “will cease.” This would mean that prudent people will cease, that is, that there will be no more prudent people. Alternate translation: \[will cease] or \[will not be found]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Have Insight (ID: `keyterm:HaveInsight`)

## Amos 5:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_t8ho)

דִּרְשׁוּ־ט֥וֹב וְ⁠אַל־רָ֖ע

**"Seek good, and not evil"**

Amos is speaking of trying to do something good or evil as if **good** and **evil** were objects someone would actually go search for or **seek**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Try to do what is good, and not what is evil]

כֵ֞ן

The word translated as **thus** indicates that what follows is a result of what came before. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that what follows is a result of what came before. Alternate translation: \[as a result,]

**"God of Armies"**

**"and establish justice in the gate"**

אוּלַ֗י \& יְהוָ֥ה

**"perhaps Yahweh"**

This part of the verse introduces the possible consequence of fulfilling the condition proposed in the previous part. אֱלֹהֵֽי־צְבָא֖וֹת

## Amos 5:15 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_etx9)

אוּלַ֗י יֶֽחֱנַ֛ן יְהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהֵֽי־צְבָא֖וֹת

**"perhaps Yahweh, God of Armies, will be gracious"**

Amos is presenting a possible situation, that Yahweh **will be gracious** to the survivors of the northern kingdom if the people hate evil, love good, and establish justice. Use a natural form in your language for introducing a condition that could happen. Alternate translation: \[if you do these things, then perhaps Yahweh, God of Armies, may be gracious]

See: Connect — Hypothetical Conditions

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:15
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Hypothetical Conditions (ID: `33327@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:15 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_ybgb)

שְׁאֵרִ֥ית יוֹסֵֽף

**"to the remnant of Joseph"**

Amos is using the possessive form to describe a **remnant** that is made up of the remaining descendants of **Joseph**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[to those who survive among the descendants of Joseph]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:15
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 5:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_u0y9)

הוֹ־ה֑וֹ

**"Alas! Alas"**

Yahweh is describing people using a word, **Alas**, that expresses a strong feeling of dismay and distress. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[Oh no! Oh no!] or \[What a disaster!]

See: Exclamations

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:16
* **Associated Articles:** Exclamations (ID: `33270@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:16 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_m00v)

וּ⁠מִסְפֵּ֖ד אֶל־י֥וֹדְעֵי נֶֽהִי

**"and wailing to those knowing a lament"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context of the previous phrase if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and they will call those knowing a lament to wailing]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:16
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_au25)

אֶעֱבֹ֥ר בְּ⁠קִרְבְּ⁠ךָ֖

**"I will pass through your midst"**

Yahweh assumes that readers would understand that the words **pass through** meant that he would be coming among them to punish them. You could say that explicitly if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[I will come among you and punish you]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:17
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Amos 5:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_rr2f)

אָמַ֥ר יְהוָֽה

**"says Yahweh"**

Amos is using the phrase **says Yahweh** to indicate that what has been said is a direct quotation from Yahweh and comes with Yahweh’s authority. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:17
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Amos 5:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_ocw7)

ה֥וֹי הַ⁠מִּתְאַוִּ֖ים

**"Woe to the ones desiring"**

Amos is using a phrase, **Woe to**, that expresses a strong feeling of grief and warning about coming disaster. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[How dreadful it will be for those desiring]

See: Exclamations

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:18
* **Associated Articles:** Exclamations (ID: `33270@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:18 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_rjcs)

אֶת־י֣וֹם יְהוָ֑ה

**"the day of Yahweh"**

Amos knows that his readers thought of **the day of Yahweh** as a time when Yahweh will come to bring justice but they thought it was to judge their enemies, not themselves. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the time when Yahweh will come to bring justice]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:18
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:18 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_i5pf)

לָ⁠מָּה־זֶּ֥ה לָ⁠כֶ֛ם י֥וֹם יְהוָ֖ה

**"What {is} this to you, the day of Yahweh"**

Amos is using the question form to rebuke his hearers for wanting **the day of Yahweh** to come. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[The day of Yahweh will bring you no benefit!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:18
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:18 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_f2d6)

הוּא־חֹ֥שֶׁךְ וְ⁠לֹא־אֽוֹר

**"It {is} darkness and not light"**

Amos is speaking as if disaster were **darkness** and deliverance were **light**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[It is a day of disaster and not of deliverance]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:18
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:18 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_wk14)

הוּא־חֹ֥שֶׁךְ וְ⁠לֹא־אֽוֹר

**"It {is} darkness and not light"**

The pronoun **It** refers to **the day of Yahweh**. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[That day is darkness and not light]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:18
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 5:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_en6n)

כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁ֨ר יָנ֥וּס אִישׁ֙ מִ⁠פְּנֵ֣י הָ⁠אֲרִ֔י

**"As when a man flees from the face of the lion"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a man** who escapes one deadly danger runs straight into another, so the people of Israel will find no escape when Yahweh judges them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[You will be as unable to escape as a man who flees from the face of the lion]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:19
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bear (ID: `fauna:Bear`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Amos 5:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_twix)

מִ⁠פְּנֵ֣י הָ⁠אֲרִ֔י

**"from the face of the lion"**

Here, **the face** represents “the presence.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[from the presence of the lion]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 5:19
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bear (ID: `fauna:Bear`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Amos 5:19 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_spa5)

וּ⁠פְגָע֖⁠וֹ הַ⁠דֹּ֑ב

**"and the bear meets him"**

## Amos - Chapter 6 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_p2d0)

Amos 6 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is a woe oracle against Israel’s complacent leaders (verses 1–7\) and Yahweh’s sworn judgment (verses 8–14\).

1. Yahweh warns the complacent and self\-confident (1–7\)
2. Yahweh rejects Israel’s pride and luxury (8–11\)
3. Yahweh brings judgment on Israel for its injustice (12–14\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Woe

A woe oracle announces coming disaster in the tone of a funeral lament.

### Oath

In verse 8 Yahweh swears by his own soul, since there is no one greater.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Chains of participles

The dashes in verses 3–6 and 13 mark phrases describing the people of verse 1\.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:0


## Amos 6:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_o1d4)

ה֚וֹי הַ⁠שַּׁאֲנַנִּ֣ים בְּ⁠צִיּ֔וֹן וְ⁠הַ⁠בֹּטְחִ֖ים בְּ⁠הַ֣ר שֹׁמְר֑וֹן

**"Woe to the ones at ease in Zion, and {to} the ones trusting in the mountain of Samaria"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[Woe to the ones who feel completely secure in Zion and in Samaria]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:1
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Trust (ID: `keyterm:LeanOn`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

ה֚וֹי

**"Woe to"**

Amos is using the word **Woe** to express a strong feeling of grief and to warn of coming disaster. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[Great disaster will come upon]

See: Exclamations

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:1

## Amos 6:1 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_i8by)

בֵּ֥ית יִשְׂרָאֵֽל

**"the house of Israel"**

Here, **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the house of Israel** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the nation of Israel]

## Amos 6:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_mnyt)

הֲ⁠טוֹבִים֙ מִן־הַ⁠מַּמְלָכ֣וֹת הָ⁠אֵ֔לֶּה

**"Are} they better than these kingdoms"**

The kingdoms of Judah (represented by Zion) and Israel (represented by Samaria) are being compared to the kingdoms represented by the cities named in this verse. Because of the parallel structure of the two questions in this verse, it is most likely that **they** refers to the cities that have just been named, and that **these kingdoms** refers to Judah and Israel. Alternate translation: \[Are those cities better than your kingdoms]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:2
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Calneh (ID: `place:Calneh`); Hamath (ID: `place:Hamath`); Gath (ID: `place:Gath`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`)

## Amos 6:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_pb99)

הֲ⁠טוֹבִים֙ מִן־הַ⁠מַּמְלָכ֣וֹת הָ⁠אֵ֔לֶּה אִם־רַ֥ב גְּבוּלָ֖⁠ם מִ⁠גְּבֻלְ⁠כֶֽם

**"Are} they better than these kingdoms? Or {is} their border greater than your border"**

The expected answer to these questions is “No.” The speaker is using the question form to emphasize that Judah and Israel are **better** in the sense that they are larger and more prosperous than the nations around them. It is possible that Amos is quoting these words from the “designated ones” who are boasting about their kingdoms. But since he refers to **your border**, it seems more likely that Amos is speaking to the “designated ones” and pointing out that, although God has made their kingdoms great, he will also destroy them because they are not better in the way that they should be. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate these questions as exclamations or as statements, as in the UST.

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:2
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Calneh (ID: `place:Calneh`); Hamath (ID: `place:Hamath`); Gath (ID: `place:Gath`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`)

## Amos 6:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_gsev)

הַֽ⁠מְנַדִּ֖ים לְ⁠י֣וֹם רָ֑ע וַ⁠תַּגִּישׁ֖וּ⁠ן

**"the ones putting far off the evil day, but you bring near"**

Amos begins by speaking about these people in the third person as **the ones putting**, and then he addresses them directly in the second person with **you bring near**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use the second person throughout. Alternate translation: \[you who put far off the evil day, but bring near]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 6:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_uit1)

הַֽ⁠מְנַדִּ֖ים לְ⁠י֣וֹם רָ֑ע

**"the ones putting far off the evil day"**

This phrase resumes the description of the people whom Amos pronounced disaster or “woe” upon in 6:1, and that description continues through verse 6\. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to repeat the statement about the woe together with the information about these people and beginning a new sentence here. Alternate translation: \[Woe to you! You put far off the evil day]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 6:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_gibp)

הַֽ⁠מְנַדִּ֖ים לְ⁠י֣וֹם רָ֑ע

**"the ones putting far off the evil day"**

Amos is speaking of these people refusing to think about the coming day of judgment as if that day were an object that they were **putting far off**, or physically pushing away from themselves. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the ones pushing away the thought of the evil day] or \[the ones refusing to think about the coming day of disaster]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 6:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_ri1f)

לְ⁠י֣וֹם רָ֑ע

**"the evil day"**

Here, Amos is referring to “the day of Yahweh” mentioned in 5:18 as **the evil day** since it was the time when Yahweh will bring justice by punishing them. Alternate translation: \[the day of disaster that Yahweh will bring]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Amos 6:3 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_uhxd)

וַ⁠תַּגִּישׁ֖וּ⁠ן

**"but you bring near"**

Here Amos is speaking hastening the coming time of violent rule as if that time were an object that could be **brought** physically **near**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[but you are hastening the arrival of a time of violence] or \[but you are making the time when violent people will rule come sooner]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 6:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_y3kn)

נִשְׁבַּע֩ אֲדֹנָ֨⁠י יְהוִ֜ה בְּ⁠נַפְשׁ֗⁠וֹ

**"The Lord Yahweh has sworn by his soul"**

Following the custom of the culture, Yahweh is swearing an oath and guaranteeing it by something that he holds sacred, in this case by **his soul** or his own life, since there is no one greater by whom he could swear. Use a natural way in your language to express an oath. Alternate translation: \[The Lord Yahweh has taken an oath, swearing by his own life]

See: Oath Formulas

## Amos 6:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_epbd)

וּ⁠נְשָׂא֞⁠וֹ דּוֹד֣⁠וֹ וּ⁠מְסָרְפ֗⁠וֹ

**"And his kinsman and the one burning him will lift him up"**

The pronoun **his** refers to a man who has died in the house mentioned in the previous verse, and the pronoun **him** refers to that same dead man. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And a dead man’s kinsman, the one burning that dead man’s body, will lift him up]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 6:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_m2v2)

וּ⁠מְסָרְפ֗⁠וֹ

**"and the one burning him"**

You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[and the one who will cremate his body instead of burying it]

הַ⁠בַּ֥יִת הַ⁠גָּד֖וֹל רְסִיסִ֑ים וְ⁠הַ⁠בַּ֥יִת הַ⁠קָּטֹ֖ן

## Amos 6:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_vps5)

הַ⁠יְרֻצ֤וּ⁠ן בַּ⁠סֶּ֨לַע֙ סוּסִ֔ים אִֽם־יַחֲר֖וֹשׁ בַּ⁠בְּקָרִ֑ים

**"Do horses run on the cliff? Or does one plow {it} with oxen"**

Amos is using the question form to show that what the people of Israel have done is even more absurd than **horses** running or **oxen** plowing on a **cliff**. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Horses certainly do not run on the cliff! No one plows it with oxen!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 6:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_u9tu)

הֲפַכְתֶּ֤ם לְ⁠רֹאשׁ֙ מִשְׁפָּ֔ט וּ⁠פְרִ֥י צְדָקָ֖ה לְ⁠לַעֲנָֽה

**"you have turned justice into poisonweed, and the fruit of righteousness into wormwood"**

Amos is speaking as if justice and righteousness were crops that these people had turned into the bitter and poisonous plants **poisonweed** and **wormwood**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[you have made justice something deadly, and the results of right behavior something bitter]

See: Metaphor

## Amos 6:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_m6n3)

לְ⁠לֹ֣א דָבָ֑ר

**"over Lo Debar"**

**Lo Debar** is the name of a town. It means, “not a thing.” So although this was a real town that Israel had captured, Amos is also saying that they are rejoicing over nothing. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could include this information in a footnote.

See: How to Translate Names

**"Have we not by our strength taken Karnaim for ourselves"**

These people are using the question form to boast about their own strength. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Indeed, by our own strength we have taken Karnaim for ourselves!]

## Amos 6:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_kp8p)

קַרְנָֽיִם

**"Karnaim"**

**Karnaim** is the name of a town. It means, “a pair of horns,” which is a symbol of strength. Amos is implying that just as the strength of Karnaim did not help them, neither should Israel trust in their own strength. Only Yahweh is truly strong. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could include this information in a footnote.

## Amos - Chapter 7 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_peqc)

Amos 7 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Three vision reports, each opening with the same formula, are followed by the narrative of Amaziah’s confrontation with Amos at Bethel (verses 10–17\).

1. Yahweh shows mercy through Amos’s intercession (1–6\)
2. Yahweh measures Israel’s faithfulness and announces judgment (7–9\)
3. Amos faces opposition for proclaiming Yahweh’s word (10–17\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Prophetic intercession

Amos pleads for Israel after the first two visions and Yahweh relents; after the third vision he does not plead.

### The royal sanctuary at Bethel

Bethel was the king’s own sanctuary, so Amaziah treats the message of Amos as a crime against Jeroboam.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Visions

Each vision repeats some form of this formula: “Thus the Lord Yahweh made me see, and behold….” Notice that in this context, “behold” quite literally means “and see” or “I saw that” since it is a vision that Yahweh is showing Amos. Translate these repeated phrases consistently.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:0


## Amos 7:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_naqg)

וְ⁠הִ֨נֵּה

**"And behold"**

Amos is using the term **behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[It was significant that]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Locust (ID: `fauna:Locust`)

## Amos 7:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_l0x0)

אַחַ֖ר גִּזֵּ֥י הַ⁠מֶּֽלֶךְ

**"was} after the mowings of the king"**

The phrase **after the mowings of the king** means that the king had the right to take the first cutting of hay from the fields for his own use, so the crop that the locusts were about to eat was all that was left for the people to use for themselves and their animals. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[after the king’s men had cut the first hay for him, so it was the crop the people needed for themselves and their animals]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Locust (ID: `fauna:Locust`)

כִּלָּה֙

**"it had finished"**

The pronoun **it** refers to the locust swarm mentioned in the previous verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[the locust swarm had finished]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:2
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

מִ֥י יָק֖וּם יַֽעֲקֹ֑ב

**"How will Jacob stand"**

Amos is using the question form for emphasis, to express his conviction that the people of Jacob will not be able to survive such a disaster. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Jacob will surely not be able to stand!]

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:2

If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the nation of Israel]

## Amos 7:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_xn6j)

אֲדֹנָ֛⁠י נִצָּ֖ב עַל־חוֹמַ֣ת אֲנָ֑ךְ

**"the Lord was standing beside a wall of a plumb line"**

Here, **the Lord was standing beside a wall of a plumb line** represents Yahweh coming to examine his people to see whether they measure up to his standard, just as a builder examines a wall to see whether it is straight. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could include this information in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[the Lord was standing beside a wall to inspect it, ready to test his people against his standard]

See: Symbolic Language

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:7
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Language (ID: `33427@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 7:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_taty)

חוֹמַ֣ת אֲנָ֑ךְ וּ⁠בְ⁠יָד֖⁠וֹ אֲנָֽךְ

**"a wall of a plumb line, and in his hand {was} a plumb line"**

The word translated as **plumb line** could mean: (1\) a plumb line, as in the ULT and UST or (2\) a type of metal called “tin.” Alternate translation: \[a tin wall and in his hand was tin]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 7:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_dpn8)

חוֹמַ֣ת אֲנָ֑ךְ

**"a wall of a plumb line"**

Amos is using the possessive form to describe a **wall** that is characterized by being made perfectly straight by using **a plumb line**, a builder’s tool made of a string with a heavy weight at the end that hangs straight down, used to check if a wall or structure is completely vertical. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[a wall that had been made straight by using a plumb line]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:7
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

In this verse, the word **him** is singular in form, but it refers to all of the Israelites as a group. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use the plural form of “him.” Alternate translation: \[my people Israel. I will not again repeat to pass over them]

## Amos 7:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_b08g)

הִנְ⁠נִ֨י שָׂ֤ם אֲנָךְ֙ בְּ⁠קֶ֨רֶב֙ עַמִּ֣⁠י יִשְׂרָאֵ֔ל

**"Behold me placing a plumb line in the midst of my people Israel"**

Yahweh holding **a plumb line** was a symbolic action by which he showed that he was going to measure his people’s behavior. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[Behold me setting a standard by which I will measure my people Israel]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:8
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 7:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_m4ab)

הִנְ⁠נִ֨י שָׂ֤ם

**"Behold me placing"**

Yahweh is using **Behold me** as a common expression of the culture to mean that he is about to do something. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I am about to place]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 7:8 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_d1vz)

לֹֽא־אוֹסִ֥יף ע֖וֹד עֲב֥וֹר לֽ⁠וֹ

**"I will not again repeat to pass over him"**

Yahweh is using **pass over** as a common expression of the culture to mean “overlook their sin.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will no longer forgive their sins]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 7:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_h1t6)

וְ⁠נָשַׁ֨מּוּ֙ בָּמ֣וֹת יִשְׂחָ֔ק וּ⁠מִקְדְּשֵׁ֥י יִשְׂרָאֵ֖ל יֶחֱרָ֑בוּ

**"And the high places of Isaac will be deserted, and the sanctuaries of Israel will be desolate"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[And all the worship places of the Israelites will surely be left in ruins]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:9
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Stone Pine (ID: `flora:Pine`); High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`)

## Amos 7:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_dhi1)

וְ⁠נָשַׁ֨מּוּ֙ בָּמ֣וֹת יִשְׂחָ֔ק

**"And the high places of Isaac will be deserted"**

The expression **will be deserted** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And I will destroy the high places of Isaac so they will be deserted]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:9
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Stone Pine (ID: `flora:Pine`); High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`)

## Amos 7:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_e3pp)

בָּמ֣וֹת יִשְׂחָ֔ק

**"the high places of Isaac"**

Here, **the high places** represent the shrines that the Israelites built on hilltops for worshiping idols. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the idolatrous places where the Israelites worshiped]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

יִשְׂחָ֔ק

**"Isaac"**

Here, **Isaac** represents all the people of Israel, as they were all descended from Isaac. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the Israelites]

וְ⁠קַמְתִּ֛י עַל

**"and I will rise against"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of sending enemies to fight against them as if he would physically **rise**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will attack]

## Amos 7:9 (#6) (id: en_tn_AMO_ove2)

בֵּ֥ית יָרָבְעָ֖ם

**"the house of Jeroboam"**

Here, **the house of Jeroboam** represents “the royal family descended from Jeroboam.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the royal line of those descended from Jeroboam]

## Amos 7:9 (#7) (id: en_tn_AMO_mm9z)

בֶּ⁠חָֽרֶב

**"with the sword"**

Here, **the sword** represents “warfare.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[with warfare]

## Amos 7:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_xd66)

וַ⁠יִּשְׁלַ֗ח אֲמַצְיָה֙ כֹּהֵ֣ן בֵּֽית־אֵ֔ל אֶל־יָרָבְעָ֥ם מֶֽלֶךְ־יִשְׂרָאֵ֖ל

**"Then Amaziah, the priest of Bethel, sent to Jeroboam, king of Israel"**

Amos is introducing **Amaziah, the priest of Bethel** as a new participant in the story. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation.

See: Introduction of New and Old Participants

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of New and Old Participants (ID: `33421@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 7:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_uequ)

וַ⁠יִּשְׁלַ֗ח אֲמַצְיָה֙ כֹּהֵ֣ן בֵּֽית־אֵ֔ל אֶל־יָרָבְעָ֥ם

**"Then Amaziah, the priest of Bethel, sent to Jeroboam"**

Amos is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[Then Amaziah, the priest of Bethel, sent a message to Jeroboam]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 7:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_u7ln)

בֵּ֣ית יִשְׂרָאֵ֔ל

**"the house of Israel"**

Here, **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the house of Israel** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel. Alternate translation: \[the Israelites]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 7:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_mie4)

לֹא־תוּכַ֣ל הָ⁠אָ֔רֶץ לְ⁠הָכִ֖יל אֶת־כָּל־דְּבָרָֽי⁠ו

**"The land is not able to contain all of his words"**

Amaziah says **The land is not able to contain all of his words** as an extreme statement for emphasis. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[The people of this land cannot endure everything he is saying]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Amos 7:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_it3p)

כִּי־כֹה֙ אָמַ֣ר עָמ֔וֹס בַּ⁠חֶ֖רֶב יָמ֣וּת יָרָבְעָ֑ם

**"For thus Amos says, ‘By the sword Jeroboam will die"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[For Amos says that Jeroboam will die by the sword]

See: Quotes within Quotes

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:11
* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`)

## Amos 7:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_zm7c)

בַּ⁠חֶ֖רֶב יָמ֣וּת יָרָבְעָ֑ם

**"By the sword Jeroboam will die"**

Here, **the sword** represents violent death in battle. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[Jeroboam will die violently in battle]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:11
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`)

## Amos 7:11 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_urrq)

וְ⁠יִ֨שְׂרָאֵ֔ל

**"and Israel"**

Here, **Israel** represents the nation of Israel. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and the Israelites]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:11
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 7:11 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_vc71)

גָּלֹ֥ה יִגְלֶ֖ה

**"going into exile, will go into exile"**

Amos is repeating forms of the verb **go into exile** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[will certainly be taken far away into exile]

See: Reduplication

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jeroboam (ID: `person:Jeroboam.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`)

## Amos 7:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_esyf)

וֶ⁠אֱכָל־שָׁ֣ם לֶ֔חֶם

**"and eat bread there"**

Amaziah is using **eat bread there** as a common expression of the culture to mean to earn what one needs to be able to eat. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and make your living there]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:12
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`)

## Amos 7:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_thqt)

וַ⁠יֹּ֤אמֶר אֲמַצְיָה֙ אֶל־עָמ֔וֹס

**"And Amaziah said to Amos"**

Even though Amos has been speaking of himself in the first person as “I” and “me”, here he switches to speaking of himself in the third person as **Amos** to recount the exchange with Amaziah. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person for consistency. Alternate translation: \[And Amaziah said to me]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:12
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 7:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_lsw9)

וּ⁠בֵֽית־אֵ֔ל לֹֽא־תוֹסִ֥יף ע֖וֹד לְ⁠הִנָּבֵ֑א כִּ֤י מִקְדַּשׁ־מֶ֨לֶךְ֙ ה֔וּא וּ⁠בֵ֥ית מַמְלָכָ֖ה הֽוּא

**"But {at} Bethel you shall not repeat to prophesy again, for it {is} a sanctuary of the king, and it {is} a house of a kingdom"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you could reverse the order of these phrases, since the second phrase gives the reason for the result that the first phrase describes. Alternate translation: \[But because it is a sanctuary of the king and it is a house of a kingdom, at Bethel you shall not repeat to prophesy again]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Amos 7:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_gmmm)

לֹֽא־תוֹסִ֥יף ע֖וֹד לְ⁠הִנָּבֵ֑א

**"you shall not repeat to prophesy again"**

Amaziah is using **you shall not repeat to prophesy again** as a common expression of the culture to mean “you must not prophesy any more.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[you must never prophesy any more]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Amos 7:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_scxu)

וּ⁠בֵ֥ית מַמְלָכָ֖ה הֽוּא

**"and it {is} a house of a kingdom"**

The implication is that by using the phrase**a house of a kingdom** which implies a royal temple, Amaziah is claiming that Bethel is a temple of the northern kingdom of Israel. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[and it is the royal temple of the northern kingdom of Israel]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Amos 7:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_rw3f)

וַ⁠יַּ֤עַן עָמוֹס֙ וַ⁠יֹּ֣אמֶר אֶל־אֲמַצְיָ֔ה

**"And Amos answered and said to Amaziah"**

As in verse 12, Amos is speaking of himself in the third person as **Amos**. If you chose to translate it in the first person there, you should do so here as well. Alternate translation: \[And I answered and said to Amaziah]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`); Sycomore Fig (ID: `flora:SycomoreFig`)

## Amos 7:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_r2qn)

וַ⁠יַּ֤עַן עָמוֹס֙ וַ⁠יֹּ֣אמֶר

**"And Amos answered and said"**

The terms **answered and said** together introduce a direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of introducing direct quotations in your language. It may not be necessary to represent both terms explicitly as modeled in the UST.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`); Sycomore Fig (ID: `flora:SycomoreFig`)

## Amos 7:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_n2c6)

לֹא־נָבִ֣יא אָנֹ֔כִי וְ⁠לֹ֥א בֶן־נָבִ֖יא אָנֹ֑כִי כִּֽי־בוֹקֵ֥ר אָנֹ֖כִי

**"I {am} not a prophet, and I {am} not a son of a prophet, for I {am} a herdsman"**

Since this verse has no verbs in the original, this could mean: (1\) that Amos is not currently a professional prophet or part of a group of prophets but a herdsman, as modeled in the UST and UST or (2\) that Amos was a herdsman and not a prophet before Yahweh called him to prophesy. Alternate translation: \[I was not a prophet, and I was not a son of a prophet, for I was a herdsman]

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`); Sycomore Fig (ID: `flora:SycomoreFig`)

## Amos 7:14 (#4) (id: en_tn_AMO_gs59)

לֹא־נָבִ֣יא אָנֹ֔כִי וְ⁠לֹ֥א בֶן־נָבִ֖יא אָנֹ֑כִי

**"I {am} not a prophet, and I {am} not a son of a prophet"**

Amos is using **a son of a prophet** as a common expression of the culture to mean “a disciple of a prophet.” He is making a point that he did not make his living as a prophet as those who were considered part of the official group of prophets did. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly as modeled in the UST.

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`); Sycomore Fig (ID: `flora:SycomoreFig`)

## Amos 7:14 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_htty)

שִׁקְמִֽים

**"sycamore figs"**

The term **sycamore figs** describes a kind of fruit that grows on a large tree that was common in the warm, low\-lying areas of that region. If your readers would not be familiar with what sycamore figs are, you could use the name of a similar fruit that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[a kind of fig]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); Amaziah (ID: `person:Amaziah.4`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`); Sycomore Fig (ID: `flora:SycomoreFig`)

## Amos 7:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_wsr3)

וַ⁠יִּקָּחֵ֣⁠נִי יְהוָ֔ה מֵ⁠אַחֲרֵ֖י הַ⁠צֹּ֑אן

**"But Yahweh took me from after the flock"**

Amos is using **took me from after the flock** as a common expression of the culture to mean “took me away from tending sheep.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[But Yahweh took me away from my work of caring for the sheep]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Amos 7:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_whvh)

## Amos 7:17 (#5) (id: en_tn_AMO_m2ir)

וְ⁠אַתָּ֗ה \& תָּמ֔וּת

**"and you, you will die"**

Amos is stating the pronoun **you** separately, even though the verb translated as **you will die** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that he is now address Amaziah’s own fate. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[and you, yourself will die]

## Amos - Chapter 8 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_ns12)

Amos 8 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Yahweh announces the end of Israel (1–3\)
2. Yahweh condemns Israel’s greed and injustice (4–8\)
3. Yahweh brings darkness and famine for his word (9–14\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The new moon and the Sabbath

Israelites did not trade on these holy days, so the merchants in verse 5 are impatient for them to end.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### A famine of Yahweh’s words

In verses 11–13, hunger and thirst are used figuratively for Yahweh withholding his word.

### Vision

This chapter begins with another vision. Be sure use language consistent with introducing the visions in the previous chapter, “Thus the Lord Yahweh made me see, and behold….” Notice that in this context, “behold” quite literally means “and see” or “I saw that” since it is a vision that Yahweh is showing Amos.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:0


## Amos 8:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_eg6c)

קָ֑יִץ

**"summer fruit"**

The word translated as **summer fruit** sounds very much like the word for **end**. Yahweh’s purpose in showing Amos summer fruit was to give him a prophecy of the end of his people. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could put this information in a footnote.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:2
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Amos (ID: `person:Amos.3`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Basket (ID: `realia:Basket`)

בַּ⁠יּ֣וֹם הַ⁠ה֔וּא

The phrase **in that day** refers to that time when Yahweh will punish them, also referred to as “the day of Yahweh” in chapter 5\. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[at the time when I punish you]

## Amos 8:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_epwo)

נְאֻ֖ם אֲדֹנָ֣⁠י יְהוִ֑ה

**"the declaration of the Lord Yahweh"**

Amos, who is conveying Yahweh’s words here, is interjecting **the declaration of the Lord Yahweh** to emphasize that what has been said is a direct quotation from Yahweh and comes with Yahweh’s authority. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

ענוי־אָֽרֶץ

**"the afflicted of the land"**

## Amos 8:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_k0jy)

מָתַ֞י יַעֲבֹ֤ר הַ⁠חֹ֨דֶשׁ֙ וְ⁠נַשְׁבִּ֣ירָה שֶּׁ֔בֶר וְ⁠הַ⁠שַּׁבָּ֖ת וְ⁠נִפְתְּחָה־בָּ֑ר

**"When will the new moon pass, that we may sell grain, and the Sabbath, that we may open wheat"**

The implication is that the Israelites were not permitted to buy and sell on the festival of **the new moon** or on **the Sabbath**, and so these merchants were impatient for those days to end. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[When will the new moon festival, on which we are not allowed to trade, be over, so that we may sell grain, and when will the Sabbath, on which we are also forbidden to trade, be over, so that we may open our bins of wheat]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); New Moon Festival (ID: `keyterm:NewMoonFestival`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Balance (ID: `realia:Balance`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`); Shekel (ID: `realia:Shekel`)

## Amos 8:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_rde7)

לְ⁠הַקְטִ֤ין אֵיפָה֙ וּ⁠לְ⁠הַגְדִּ֣יל שֶׁ֔קֶל

**"to make the ephah small and to make the shekel great"**

The implication is that these merchants would measure out grain in a container smaller than a standard **ephah** and weigh their customers’ silver with a weight heavier than a standard **shekel**, so that they cheated the people who bought from them. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[to use an undersized ephah measure for grain and an oversized shekel weight for silver]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

מֹאזְנֵ֥י מִרְמָֽה

**"balances of deceit"**

Amos is using the possessive form to describe **balances** that are characterized by **deceit**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[deceitful balances]

## Amos 8:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_wtz9)

לִ⁠קְנ֤וֹת בַּ⁠כֶּ֨סֶף֙ דַּלִּ֔ים וְ⁠אֶבְי֖וֹן בַּ⁠עֲב֣וּר נַעֲלָ֑יִם

**"to buy the poor with silver and the needy in exchange for a pair of sandals"**

The merchants are leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[to buy the poor as slaves with silver and to buy the needy as slaves in exchange for a pair of sandals]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:6
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Amos 8:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_enbd)

בַּ⁠כֶּ֨סֶף֙ דַּלִּ֔ים וְ⁠אֶבְי֖וֹן

**"the poor with silver and the needy"**

The merchants are using the adjectives **poor** and **needy** as nouns to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate these adjectives with equivalent phrases. Alternate translation: \[poor people with silver and needy people]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:6
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Amos 8:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_ud0p)

וְ⁠אֶבְי֖וֹן בַּ⁠עֲב֣וּר נַעֲלָ֑יִם

**"and the needy in exchange for a pair of sandals"**

The implication is that **a pair of sandals** would only cost a very small amount. This means that they were buying needy people as slaves for a price as low as the price of a pair of sandals. You could say that explicitly if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[and the needy for as little as the price of a pair of sandals]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:6
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Amos 8:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_by14)

בִּ⁠גְא֣וֹן יַעֲקֹ֑ב

**"by the pride of Jacob"**

As in 6:8, **the pride of Jacob** could refer to: (1\) the land of Israel, since that is its meaning elsewhere, and it would make no sense to swear by something negative, such as sinful pride. Alternate translation: \[by this land that Jacob’s people are so proud of] or (2\) Yahweh himself. This would then be equivalent to the phrase in 6:8, “The Lord Yahweh has sworn by his soul.” Alternate translation: \[by himself, of whom Jacob’s people are so proud]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 8:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_w4xq)

יַעֲקֹ֑ב

**"Jacob"**

Here, **Jacob** represents the descendants of Jacob, the people of Israel. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[Jacob’s descendants]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 8:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_k79m)

אִם־אֶשְׁכַּ֥ח לָ⁠נֶ֖צַח כָּל־מַעֲשֵׂי⁠הֶֽם

**"If I ever forget all of their deeds"**

Following the custom of the culture, Yahweh is swearing an oath by stating the first part of a condition (“if”) but not the second part (“then”), which would be the penalty for not telling the truth. Normally the penalty would be understood to be something general such as “may Yahweh punish me severely,” yielding something like, “If I ever forget…, then may Yahweh punish me severely…” But since Yahweh is speaking, stating this would not make sense. It may be clearest to translate the oath as a positive statement, as in the UST.

See: Oath Formulas

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 8:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_wstf)

הַ֤⁠עַל זֹאת֙ לֹֽא־תִרְגַּ֣ז הָ⁠אָ֔רֶץ וְ⁠אָבַ֖ל כָּל־יוֹשֵׁ֣ב בָּ֑⁠הּ

**"Will not the land tremble over this, and everyone dwelling in it mourn"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize that the land will certainly tremble and that everyone living in it will certainly mourn. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[The land will surely tremble over this, and everyone dwelling in it will mourn!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Nile River (ID: `place:Nile`)

## Amos 8:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_ynx2)

זֹאת֙

**"this"**

The pronoun **this** refers to the deeds of the people that Yahweh mentioned in the previous verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[will not the land tremble over the evil deeds that the people have done]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Nile River (ID: `place:Nile`)

## Amos 8:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_vf34)

וְ⁠עָלְתָ֤ה כָ⁠אֹר֙ כֻּלָּ֔⁠הּ וְ⁠נִגְרְשָׁ֥ה ו⁠נשקה כִּ⁠יא֥וֹר מִצְרָֽיִם

**"And all of it will rise like the Nile, and be cast up and sunk, like the Nile of Egypt"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the Nile of Egypt** rises in flood and then sinks back down again, so the whole land of Israel will heave up and down. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[And the whole land will heave upward and then settle back down, just as the Nile of Egypt rises in flood and then recedes]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Nile River (ID: `place:Nile`)

## Amos 8:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_afkm)

בַּ⁠יּ֣וֹם הַ⁠ה֗וּא

**"in that day"**

The phrase **in that day** refers to that time when Yahweh will punish them. See how you translated this same phrase in verse 3\.

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 8:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_h47t)

נְאֻם֙ אֲדֹנָ֣⁠י

**"the declaration of the Lord"**

Amos, who is conveying Yahweh’s words here, is interjecting **the declaration of the Lord** to emphasize that what has been said is a direct quotation from Yahweh and comes with Yahweh’s authority. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:9
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 8:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_AMO_kdih)

וְ⁠הֵבֵאתִ֥י הַ⁠שֶּׁ֖מֶשׁ בַּֽ⁠צָּהֳרָ֑יִם וְ⁠הַחֲשַׁכְתִּ֥י לָ⁠אָ֖רֶץ בְּ⁠י֥וֹם אֽוֹר

**"that I will make the sun set at noon, and I will darken the land in a day of light"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could connect the phrases with a word that shows that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional as in the UST. Another approach would be to combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[that I will make the sunlight completely disappear in the middle of the day]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:9
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Amos 8:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_i3jc)

וְ⁠הַעֲלֵיתִ֤י עַל־כָּל־מָתְנַ֨יִם֙ שָׂ֔ק וְ⁠עַל־כָּל־רֹ֖אשׁ קָרְחָ֑ה

**"And I will bring up sackcloth on all loins, and baldness on every head"**

Wearing **sackcloth** and shaving the head were a symbolic actions by which Yahweh’s people would show that they were mourning and grieving. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[And I will cause everyone to put on sackcloth around their waists and to shave their heads bald to show their grief]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:10
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

## Amos 8:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_z4qe)

וְ⁠שַׂמְתִּ֨י⁠הָ֙ כְּ⁠אֵ֣בֶל יָחִ֔יד וְ⁠אַחֲרִיתָ֖⁠הּ כְּ⁠י֥וֹם מָֽר

**"And I will make it like the mourning for an only child, and its end like a bitter day"**

The point of these comparisons is that just as the death of an **only child** brings parents the deepest possible grief, and just as **a bitter day** brings the greatest sorrow, so the people of Israel will grieve when Yahweh punishes them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[And I will cause you to grieve as deeply as parents who mourn the death of their only child, and everything will end in the most bitter sorrow]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 8:10
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

רָעָ֖ב בָּ⁠אָ֑רֶץ לֹֽא־רָעָ֤ב לַ⁠לֶּ֨חֶם֙ וְ⁠לֹֽא־צָמָ֣א לַ⁠מַּ֔יִם כִּ֣י אִם־לִ⁠שְׁמֹ֔עַ אֵ֖ת דִּבְרֵ֥י יְהוָֽה

**"a famine in the land, not a famine for bread, and not a thirst for water, but rather to hear the words of Yahweh"**

## Amos - Chapter 9 Introduction (id: en_tn_AMO_m61x)

Amos 9 Chapter Introduction
===========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Yahweh brings judgment on Israel (1–6\)
2. Yahweh judges Israel among the nations (7–10\)
3. Yahweh restores David’s fallen house and blesses his people (11–15\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The booth of David

The fallen booth of David in 9:11 pictures his ruined dynasty, which Yahweh will rebuild.

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 9:0


## Amos 9:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_xw0f)

רָאִ֨יתִי אֶת־אֲדֹנָ֜⁠י נִצָּ֣ב עַֽל־הַ⁠מִּזְבֵּ֗חַ

**"I saw the Lord standing by the altar"**

Amos is using the words translated as **I saw the Lord standing by the altar** to introduce a new vision that Yahweh showed him. Use a word or phrase in your language to make that clear.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 9:1
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Capital (ID: `realia:Capital`); Doorstep (ID: `realia:Doorstep`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`)

## Amos 9:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_AMO_c6x1)

הַ⁠כַּפְתּ֜וֹר

**"the capital"**

A **capital** is the block of stone at the top of a column or pillar, on which the roof of a building rests. If your readers would not be familiar with what a capital is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[the top of the temple columns]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Amos 9:1
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. ## Amos 9:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_AMO_raxv)

יָדִ֣⁠י תִקָּחֵ֑⁠ם

**"my hand will take them"**

Here, **my hand** represents the power of the Lord to act. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[I will powerfully take them]

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)
